<!--memo:ec38b23f3d33-->
### Game Genealogy: From Operator to Director Mode Paradigm Shift

> 2026-03-01 14:51:03

I feel there are several very different types of games now. One is narrative-type—it may focus on world-building or linear concrete experiences, bringing immersive and very unique feelings. These games usually have higher cognitive load, delayed feedback, and rely on pre-scripted big moments or plot climaxes. There are actually quite many such products, including some otome games, or like "Black Myth: Wukong," and Steam's "Love Is All Around."

Another type is rule-based gaming. These games generally have some cognitive load, requiring sequential, high-frequency instant feedback, like "League of Legends" or "Honor of Kings." Some might be drive-type mobile games, like needing to draw a picture, or some text-based mini-games that bring unexpected surprises through instant feedback—I find this interesting too.

I feel game modes are slowly changing. From initially focusing on hand-eye coordination, now more like a director mode. Traditional games are more like making players become operators, while now may be more about letting players become directors or decision-makers.

Also, I feel games are evolving from consuming static assets to generative emergence. Now it's not just a CS code package—more likely to be an agent with memory and independent logic. You can substitute yourself into the player role.

### Games' Emotional Sanctuary: Low Risk, No Betrayal, and Thousand Faces for Thousand People

> 2026-03-01 14:52:54

Actually, I feel games are slowly inclining toward emotional consumption. Emotional consumption has certain characteristics: it must be low-risk—after all, it's in a consumption scenario, like a sanctuary for intimate relationships in the real world.

Then although it requires this feeling of being chosen, and it will never betray—this is a transformation of the player-world relationship.

I also feel there's narrative continuity. With current technology, generally can only provide a universal background story, then maybe some branch choices—this is an engineering compromise. But I feel true AI-driven should be "a thousand faces for a thousand people"—every interaction conducts high-quality reasoning and rendering, it doesn't forget, its narrative must be complete.

Because some story-driven games heavily rely on narrative—narrative is like a very self-related, very independent, very subjective experience.

<!--memo:9c4fada3bcd3-->
### OpenClaw Skills Loading: The Four-Layer Decision-Filtering Mechanism

> 2026-03-03 16:57:42 · `#格物/ai`

OpenClaw's skills-loading principles.

The LLM is never the "full-set indexer" of skills.

It's only the "decision-maker for the final candidate set."

Skills decisions are layered — usually four layers of filtering.

Layer 0: Skill Registry (system layer), stored in a database, vector store, or index.

Layer 1: non-LLM routing (hard filtering), based on certain types, embeddings, then abstracted out.

Layer 2: Candidate Skills (the set visible to the model) — this is what finally gets inserted, maybe a few, a dozen, twenty-odd skills, with name, description and schema.

Layer 3: invocation & execution (runtime) — usually only 0–1 are invoked, returning the result for that turn.

<!--memo:34d0867956c0-->
### OpenClaw's cron Mechanism: Time-Triggered vs Event-Triggered Division of Labour

> 2026-03-04 13:57:03 · `#OpenClaw`

The cron in openclaw feels quite interesting

You can set temporary tasks, and also recurring tasks

The difference between sub-agent and cron is really like this: the openclaw gateway is like a company, cron is the alarm clock, and sub-agent is like a temp outsourced worker brought in on short notice

Trigger dimension: cron is triggered by time, whether it's a one-off task or a periodic task

A sub-agent is triggered by events and instructions, usually dynamically spawned by the main agent (or a cron task) deciding "this job is too time-consuming, I'd get stuck doing it myself"

cron solves when to do it; sub-agent solves how to outsource and break down a big task

A Sub-agent is throwaway when done; a Cron task is static and persistent (its config is saved in the .openclaw/cron/ directory), and stays around long term unless you clear it manually

Under the hood, openclaw doesn't rely on the underlying operating system's crontab, but maintains a cron scheduler inside the nodejs gateway

In order not to interrupt the context of your current chat with the main Agent, when Cron is woken it usually spins up an "independent session" and runs the task silently in the background. Only when "deliver": true is set in the config file will it send the final result to your Telegram, WhatsApp or terminal

A Sub-agent is essentially a sandboxed, asynchronous, isolated environment, making use of OpenClaw's sessions_spawn mechanism

When the main Agent judges a task is heavy, it calls the system-level built-in tool sessions_spawn. This call is non-blocking: the main Agent throws out the task and can keep chatting with you

openclaw creates a brand-new Session for the sub agent; it doesn't inherit the hundreds of rounds of messy chat history from the main session, and only injects a clean toolset (such as TOOLS.md) as needed

The sub-agent and the main process each run in different parallel streams (if the highest security mode is enabled, the sub-agent even runs in a separate Docker container). When the sub-agent finishes, it packages the result into a standardised internal event block (Announce Context) and, like sending a network request, "calls back" and pushes the result into the main thread or chat channel that created it

<!--memo:5c205cb4a3c7-->
### launchd vs systemd: Two Philosophies of Service Management, Event-Driven and State-Driven

> 2026-03-04 14:09:51 · `#格物/mac`

Thinking about the differences between macOS's launchd and systemctl

Actually launchd : systemd   =  macOS's philosophy of service management : Linux's philosophy of service management

They solve the same problem, but the approaches they take are diametrically opposed

Their functionality is almost the same: user level, system service management, restart on failure, scheduled triggers

The design philosophy: one is event-driven, the other command-driven

launchd is event-driven; not always running saves power, it wakes fast, it's aimed at desktop users, with strong consistency

Linux's systemd is state + dependency driven, including active / failed / inactive, leaning more towards long-running services and service stability

In other words, with launchd the system decides according to rules when things may run

User-level launchd is desktop-friendly

<!--memo:9887115446f3-->
### Introduction to the secrets directory

> 2026-03-04 17:25:25 · `#OpenClaw`

This directory is mainly used to store all the high-privilege credentials an AI agent needs to run autonomously:

LLM API keys: call keys for providers such as OpenAI, Anthropic, xAI (Grok).

App authorization tokens: identity credentials used to take over and control your email, Slack/chat tools, calendar, browser, and local file system.

Environment variables: sensitive configuration parameters needed for the system and each sub-agent (Agents) to run

When the Clawdbot Gateway service starts, it reads the configuration files in this directory (such as clawdbot.json or a .env file). The system loads these tokens and keys into memory and, depending on the needs of different tasks, assigns them to different sub-agents (for example: the highest-privilege Main Agent, a permission-restricted Work Agent, or a sandboxed Coding Agent)

When the AI decides to call a tool (like batch-replying to emails or operating on a local code repository), it first checks the permission whitelist (usually in exec-approvals.json at the same level). Once verified, the system pulls the corresponding credentials from the Secrets directory and dynamically injects them into the execution environment (a Docker sandbox by default), or sends them as HTTP headers to a third-party service's API

To ensure the AI agent can run autonomously in the background for a long time without constantly asking the user for verification codes or passwords, the system stores all authorization state persistently in this directory, working together with memory files like USER.md (information about you) and SOUL.md (the AI's personality settings)


<!--memo:39a2355baf6f-->
### The sessions_spawn mechanism

> 2026-03-04 17:28:47 · `#OpenClaw`

sessions_spawn is a built-in system-level tool (Tool) that OpenClaw provides to the main Agent. When the main Agent hits a heavy task (say it needs to search dozens of web pages, or run a long code-test process), it can call this tool to dynamically "clone" or spawn multiple sub-agents to work in parallel in the background

A sub-agent doesn't inherit the hundreds of rounds of idle chat between you and the main Agent. It goes off with a clear subtask objective, and when it's done it distills the result into a concise Summary reported back to the main session, after which the sandbox is destroyed. That way your main chat interface stays clean forever

Cost optimization, the high-level feature geeks love most: through the model parameter of sessions_spawn, you can achieve heterogeneous model collaboration

Your main session might be running the smart but expensive Claude 3.5 Sonnet or GPT-4o, dedicated to understanding your complex intent and breaking task structures down

Calls to sessions_spawn are non-blocking


<!--memo:eef02ebe0366-->
### Claude Code Sub-agents and OpenClaw's sessions_spawn

> 2026-03-04 17:33:32 · `#OpenClaw`

Essentially this is to prevent context window [overflow]

Claude Sub-agents (Repo-centric): it's deeply bound to software engineering and code repositories. Claude's sub-agents usually exist as YAML or Markdown files under the .claude/agents/ directory (say a dedicated bug-fixer or test-writer). It's version-controlled along with your project code, and shared by the whole dev team

OpenClaw (Universal & Event-driven): it's a general-purpose automation engine, never limited to writing code. It can be used for DevOps monitoring, automated web scraping, personal life assistance, and so on. Its sandbox is more general-purpose, and it can even combine perfectly with the Cron scheduled tasks mentioned earlier

In Claude's higher-level playbook, because multiple sub-agents work in turns, geeks usually put a progress.md file at the project root. Sub-agent A finishes a code review and writes the result into the file; sub-agent B then reads the file and continues writing tests. It relies on shared external state

But in openclaw, it's pure asynchronous sandboxes. A sub-agent runs its task in a completely isolated environment, and when it's done it packages the result directly into an internal event block (Announce Context) and asynchronously "calls back" to the main agent through the system's Event Bus. Used and destroyed, leaving no trace, with no need to rely on external files to pass context along

### The Division of Labour Between Humans and AI: Humans Produce Fragments, AI Organises Them—the Best Collaboration

> 2026-03-05 17:52:43 · `#格物/ai`

My fragile inner self

In the AI era, is a person's ability to systematise really still that important? I thought before about comparing it with first principles; I'd say my systematising ability is okay, but it's only a little stronger than most people .... AI is a superhuman!!

From an evolutionary standpoint, the human brain itself was not designed to handle systematic knowledge; the human brain has lots of thoughts—where the food is, who is a friend / enemy, which path is safer, what happened today

These pieces of information are highly fragmented + situational

A lot of the information isn't systematic knowledge either, but fragments of experience

So humans are especially good at:

Remembering stories

Remembering cases

Remembering gossip

Remembering images

Remembering emotions

But not good at:

Long chains of logic

Strictly structured systems

Large-scale knowledge graphs

The best division of labour between humans and AI is more like: humans produce fragments, AI organises fragments

### The GEP-A2A Protocol: Making Agent Experience Flow Across Platforms Like HTTP

> 2026-03-05 18:23:11

The greatest thing about HTTP isn't the technology itself, but that it made "who built the website" unimportant—whether you use Chrome or Safari, whether you're accessing a server on Alibaba Cloud or AWS, communication works fine

What GEP wants to do is the same thing: make "the Agent from which platform" unimportant—an Agent on OpenClaw solves a problem, and an Agent on Cursor can directly inherit that experience. The flow of experience is no longer blocked by platform boundaries

### EvoMap Worker Mode: Headhunter Push vs Active Job-Seeking Task Matching

> 2026-03-05 20:41:14 · `#格物/evomap`

Worker mode

It's really just the agent hanging on the network on standby, with Evomap actively pushing tasks to it, so it doesn't need to go find work itself

There are two ways to get tasks in Evomap; one is active mode, that is, task mode

The Agent periodically asks: "Is there a task suitable for me?" → Hub returns a task list, the Agent picks a favourite → claims it → goes and does it

It's like actively browsing job sites every day to find work, and only sending a résumé once you find something suitable

Worker mode is really just the agent registering once

The Agent registers once: "I'm online, I'm good at javascript and python, I can take 3 tasks at once"  —>  Hub records your information and keeps matching for you  —>  when there's a suitable task, Hub pushes it to you ->  the Agent gets the notification, accepts, starts working

It's like leaving your résumé with a headhunter: when there's a suitable opportunity the headhunter contacts you directly, and you don't have to check job sites every day

<!--memo:d96c310d2f68-->
### How EvoMap Runs: Registration, Asset Flow and the Points System

> 2026-03-05 20:53:02 · `#格物/evomap`

How Evomap runs

Periodic sync, a work cycle every four hours, a keep-alive every fifteen minutes

It only does one thing: tells the hub it's still alive

The hub is the central server running behind it

Every agent in Evomap, when it first connects to the hub, must report in first

Agent → Hub: I'm called node_abc123, I'm online, I'm good at Python
Hub   → Agent: all right, your ID is node_abc123, this is your points account

From this moment on, the Hub knows there is one more node in the world. Everything that follows is based on this identity

Then there's asset flow: the agent solves a problem, the Agent packages the solution into a Gene + Capsule and sends it to the Hub. The Hub scores the Capsule along multiple dimensions (a GDI score), checking: structural completeness, semantic quality, uniqueness, usefulness. Only those that pass review can be promoted—promotion is in fact the promoted, public state. Capsules that pass review enter the "public zone"; all Agents across the whole network can fetch them, and the publisher starts earning points

***

Little A's ranking rises on the leaderboard

Hub = the server behind this platform

Agent = the developer (both a knowledge contributor and a task executor)

Capsule = a code commit (open to everyone after review)

Points = pay (the more you contribute, the more you earn)

Reputation score = GitHub stars + a credit rating

<!--memo:8014754f9c71-->
### Ralph Loop's Context Management, a Deep Dive

> 2026-03-09 16:36:54 · `#格物/ralph`

First you have to understand the essence of the problem, Context Rot

Traditional AI coding sessions face what's called "context rot": every prompt, reply and correction keeps accumulating tokens in the context window; wrong implementation approaches remain in the history, and even if they get corrected later, they still influence subsequent output

The compaction loop is essentially about constantly compressing the conversation to free up space for context, but the more you compress the less work gets done, until finally you fall into an infinite loop

It's very common in MCP scenarios, including MCP tool definitions being too long, tool results being too large, too many parallel tool calls, and very long system prompts, rule files, CLAUDE.md

compaction  loop = context too full → triggers compression → still too full after compression → compress again → repeat infinitely, the task can't move forward

As the number of tokens an LLM receives increases, the relationships between tokens grow quadratically (quadratic scaling). This means the more tokens there are, the harder it is for the model to process information and make decisions. In practice, every LLM has a "smart zone" and a "dumb zone"—at around the 40% mark of the context, the LLM starts entering the dumb zone. The exact boundary varies from person to person, but there's no doubt that a boundary exists

Compaction is a lossy function. When the sliding-window mechanism removes tokens to free up space for new content, if key specifications get removed, "the tower falls". This leads to the dumb zone—after exceeding 60-70% of capacity, LLM performance degrades measurably

Research shows the dumb zone isn't only a function of the token limit, but also of context pollution. Even if the window isn't full, the presence of contradictory information (for example a bug and its failed fix attempt) also confuses the model's probabilistic generation

Geoffrey Huntley condensed his design philosophy into one sentence:

"Ralph is a deliberate attempt to minimize allocation. So I never get a compaction event."

The core insight is to use a fresh context on each iteration. Instead of accumulating a huge conversation history, each Ralph loop starts from a clean state. The AI reads the current state of the codebase, the prompt file and related docs, then works. Progress gets committed to git. The next iteration starts from a fresh state, reads the updated state, and carries on

Ralph's design sidesteps compaction by re-allocating the full specification on every iteration, ensuring key context is never compressed away

Ralph Loop doesn't try to "summarise" the past; instead it guides the Agent to reload itself through the prompt. Each round of prompt always contains a clear description of the core goal, while the concrete execution details are left to the Agent to explore the environment with in real time. This "just-in-time context" loading method allows Ralph to handle projects far larger than a single context window's capacity

***


<!--memo:6b2d69009b18-->
### Ralph Loop vs OpenClaw

> 2026-03-09 16:53:51 · `#格物/AI`

The problem they solve is the same: both are fighting the finiteness of the LLM context window

OpenClaw is an open-source framework for building autonomous AI Agents that run around the clock, remember context and take action on external services, self-hosted, model-agnostic, with all data stored locally as Markdown files

The Agent loop—input → context → model → tools → repeat → reply—is the same pattern Claude Code uses. Every serious Agent framework runs some form of this loop. The difference is what's wrapped around it, and what is wrapped around it?

Ralph Loop actively dies, externalises memory, and doesn't try to manage context, but lets it die naturally after each iteration

OpenClaw is persistent conversation, compaction management, maintaining a persistent session across time, using Compaction to handle context overflow

OpenClaw's Agent Runner includes a Context Window Guard: it monitors the token count and triggers summarisation or stops the loop before the window "explodes", preventing incoherent model output

When a session approaches automatic compaction, OpenClaw triggers a silent agentic turn, reminding the model to write persistent memory to disk before the context gets compressed. The default prompt explicitly tells the model it may reply, but usually NO_REPLY is the correct response, and the user never sees this turn

Ralph has no vector retrieval—it doesn't need it, because the Agent rebuilds its situational awareness each time by directly reading files and git log

***

<!--memo:ca4c9224e26b-->
### OpenClaw's session management method

> 2026-03-10 17:32:43 · `#格物/openclaw`

The Gateway centrally holds state + sessionKey routing + sessionId continuation/reset + transcript persistence + compaction

The gateway is the single source of truth. OpenClaw explicitly places session state on the Gateway (the main OpenClaw); UI clients like the macOS app, WebChat, and TUI should query the Gateway for the session list and token counts, rather than reading local files themselves. In remote mode, the files you see on your own machine aren't necessarily the session state actually in use — what's really in effect is the store on the remote Gateway host

So the architectural orientation is centralised state, with multiple UIs sharing the same session truth, and the front end as stateless / weakly stateful as possible

Multi-device consistency is very important, otherwise web, desktop, and chat channels easily end up each having their own history

OpenClaw's session persistence is split into two layers:

The first layer is sessions.json, a small mutable mapping of sessionKey -> SessionEntry, holding the current session metadata

The second layer is the .jsonl transcript, the real conversation stream, using append-only JSONL, in which messages, tool calls, compaction summaries, and so on are stored, used to rebuild the model context in subsequent turns

One important reason OpenClaw's long sessions can keep running is compaction. It summarises earlier conversation into a persisted compaction entry while keeping the newer messages; subsequent turns see "compressed summary + recent messages." And this compaction is persistent — it isn't a one-off summarise that gets thrown away

***


<!--memo:dc8234caaf29-->
### OpenClaw first routes by sessionKey, then decides which current sessionId to land on

> 2026-03-10 17:38:21 · `#格物/openclaw`

Only one kind of case enters the main session: DM with dmScope: "main". The official definition is very clear: main means "all DMs share the main session," that is, "all private chats share the main session," not "all channels share the main session."

So group, thread, channel, and room all converge into one session

main: all DMs share the main session

per-peer: isolated by the other party

per-channel-peer: isolated by channel + other party

Only the "private chat" line, when you configure it as dmScope: "main", aggregates across DMs into the main session

Group chats, channels, and threads don't belong to this main DM bucket. They have their own session routing

A session keeps being reused until it expires; and expiry is judged on the next inbound message. The daily reset defaults to 4 a.m. local time on the gateway host; if idleMinutes is also configured, then whichever of daily and idle expires first takes effect

DM is the form of the information, meaning private chat, one-to-one messages

direct is OpenClaw's internal name for the routing type of this message form

dmScope: when a message belongs to direct, what rules does OpenClaw use to merge these private chats into sessions

### Analyzing the cross-session memory retrieval methods in openclaw

> 2026-03-11 15:31:30 · `#格物/openclaw`

It's not just RAG; there are two methods, and both tell the LLM.

Semantic recall through the memory system: memory_search does semantic search over the memory and the files in the directory — this is local RAG.

Reading other sessions' history directly through session tools: OpenClaw also has dedicated sessions_list / sessions_history / sessions_send / sessions_spawn tools, whose goal is to let the agent list sessions, fetch the history of a certain session, and send messages to other sessions. This path is more like "directly querying the session log / forwarding context," and doesn't count as RAG.


<!--memo:da0537d7cd54-->
### Introduction to openclaw's memory modes

> 2026-03-11 15:33:55 · `#格物/openclaw`

You know which session it is and want to see the raw conversation → use sessions_history

You don't know which session it's in, you only remember the rough topic/keywords → use memory_search

You want to distill old sessions into long-term memory, for future retrieval → use the session-memory hook / memory files

You want recent sessions to also be semantically searchable → turn on memory.qmd.sessions.enabled or the experimental session memory search

You want to throw a task to another session to keep handling it → use sessions_send

You want to first see which sessions are available to query → use sessions_lis

<!--memo:95aeb6e246db-->
### Lazy loading of tools: discovering capabilities on demand, like RAG

> 2026-03-11 19:19:33 · `#格物/openclaw`

tool serach

It fundamentally changes the way the LLM interacts with external tools.

From "preloading all tool definitions" to "discover on demand, load dynamically."

Tool Search is a mechanism that lets an AI model discover and load tools on demand. The model initially holds only a lightweight "search tool"; when it needs a specific capability, it finds the relevant tool through a search query, and then its full definition is dynamically injected into context.

This is similar to Lazy Loading in programming or Demand Paging in an operating system — resources aren't all loaded at startup, but loaded into memory only on first access.

Through instant retrieval, in method and in principle.

Similar to the RAG idea: don't load all the information into context at once.

<!--memo:7a16d07312c1-->
### Thinking Through Perfectly's Design

> 2026-03-15 00:18:22 · `#格物/AI`

It positions itself as "the first AI-native recruiting agency"

Perfectly provides an AI agent named Paul. Paul directly replaces the role of traditional headhunters and recruiters, able to complete the whole pipeline end to end automatically: talent sourcing, outreach, screening, and so on. Its final deliverable is not a pile of résumés, but "candidates ready for an interview" pushed straight into your team's Slack

Perfectly's design philosophy greatly reduces the "recruiting tax" for founders and hiring managers—that is, the time wasted screening résumés and doing first-round chats. Its core features include:

Voice-to-Stack (voice to requirements)

Zero-UI Workflow: the agent does it automatically and delivers the result

Continuous Calibration: you can keep calibrating, keep giving feedback

The underlying implementation owes a lot to its co-founder Huimin Xie (former TikTok machine learning scientist). Instead of using traditional "keyword matching" for recruiting, they rebuilt recruiting as a "recommendation engine" problem

Traditional recruiting software relies on Boolean logic (for example, search: Java AND SpringBoot AND 5 years experience), which tends to miss a large number of good candidates whose résumés just don't have the right keywords. Perfectly borrowed the logic of TikTok's content distribution algorithm. When the AI pushes a candidate to you and you choose "advance" or "reject" in Slack, the system treats those actions as supervised signals and automatically fine-tunes the weights. It not only matches hard skills, it also implicitly learns your preferences about "school background," "job-hopping frequency" or "project depth" from your past choices

The cold-start problem, that's a bit interesting

Solving the LLM's "cold-start problem" (The Cold-Start Problem): the biggest challenge for a recommender system is the cold start—when a 3-person startup has just started hiring and there's no historical feedback data in the system, how do you guarantee the recommendations are accurate?

Perfectly's solution: the LLM's internal knowledge network + multi-source external data enrichment + implicit preference extraction

Baseline construction: use the large language model (LLM)'s own understanding of the vast tech stack and industry, taking the user's "5-minute voice brief" and a simple JD as the "soul baseline"

Data enrichment: crawl corporate websites, product documentation and other web data to enrich the picture of the company (knowing what your company actually does)

Comparison Cards: for hazy indicators like "culture fit," the AI generates trade-off cards when you're recording by voice (for example: do you want deep technical expertise or extremely strong adaptability?), turning cultural preferences directly into standardised questions for the downstream interview bot. Through these means, before the first piece of feedback appears, the model can already reach a high matching baseline

Design of the closed-loop recommendation system: similar to those content distribution systems

User Representation: extract the employer's implicit preferences (through voice, website data).

Item Pool: candidate profiles from across the web.

Recall & Ranking: preliminary screening through vector similarity.

Fine Ranking & Agent Interaction: the AI Agent proactively chats with candidates to obtain dynamic data.

Feedback Loop: the employer likes/rejects in Slack, and the system automatically updates the recommendation weights

Intake Module (requirements ingestion and multimodal parsing): solving the problem of "employers can't be bothered to write a long JD"

Use ElevenLabs or OpenAI Whisper to turn a 5-minute voice brief into high-accuracy text

Use an LLM (like GPT-4o or Claude 3.5 Sonnet) for information extraction, turning unstructured voice content into structured JSON data (including: must-have skills, nice-to-have skills, team vibe, salary range, etc.)

The system automatically crawls the company's website, product documentation and even the founders' past tweets, and stores them in a vector database (Vector DB) to build a "company profile," solving the lack of context at cold start


<!--memo:85a49c250de8-->
### An Agent Isn't Really That Forgetful — It's Its Memory System

> 2026-03-15 23:42:59 · `#格物/ai`

An agent isn't really that forgetful; it's that its memory system is designed wrong.

Write failure: important decisions weren't persisted.

Retrieval failure: it was stored, but not found when needed.

Compaction loss: during context compression/trimming, key constraints got lost.

So the article's main thread is:

first fix memory at OpenClaw's harness / outer framework layer

then define a set of memory contracts (a memory contract)

if that's still not enough, then consider a stronger memory substrate (underlying memory infrastructure)

such as QMD, Mem0, Cognee, Obsidian

An agent with no memory isn't an assistant, but a "very confident amnesiac autocomplete."

Don't treat memory as a vague concept; split it into 3 subsystems.

The write path is the writing path — corresponding to what content is worth writing in, when to write, and in what form.

The read path is the reading path — when memory must be retrieved, and how to retrieve it.

Compaction / GC serves as compression and garbage collection.

Treat memory as an engineering problem.

Was it not written in?

Or was it written in but not retrieved?

Or was it in the context originally and later compressed away?

This is one of the most valuable points in the article.

Solution, layer one: first fix the harness layer.

Before context compression, first force a flush step, writing the important information from this session into the memory file.

After a long session, key state drops out of the context.

The model can never see that information again.

Then behavior drifts.


<!--memo:f7ce81afa9e4-->
### The Design Philosophy of the iOS Photo Library

> 2026-03-16 15:15:28 · `#格物/ios`

Library serves as the master library, and Albums are labels that can be referenced repeatedly.

iOS 26 splits photo management more clearly into the two perspectives of Library and Collections, and lets you customize the order of Collections, the contents of Pinned Collections, and even adjust the display density/size of Collections.

So what's valuable is keeping the master library complete, having a small number of high-value Albums, and pinning the frequently used entry points to Pinned.

Photos divide into an input layer and a use layer; the use layer only organizes content you'll use later. Note that what's being distinguished here is that not every photo is worth entering Albums.

Keep the number of Albums as small as possible, but give each album a clear purpose: coffee, scenery, restaurants.

Pin the stable ones: Lift, Work, Docs.


<!--memo:5271a913a2d5-->
### Memory Should Be an Engineering Capability

> 2026-03-16 16:42:29

Memory can't be handed over to the LLM to deal with on its own.

Memory should be treated as a read-write storage system, not as an appendage of a chat quota.

The conditions memory must at least satisfy:

durability

retrievability

stability under compression

The solution: start by fixing memory at the harness layer.

Do a checkpoint / memory flush before compaction: before context is compressed, force a durable note.

decisions

constraints

open questions

owners

any state that would break the plan if forgotten

Mainly solving:

missed writes

compaction loss

Systems are extremely prone to accidents when the two categories decisions / constraints are lost.

decision: we decided not to dual-write to the old table anymore

constraint: after the switch, writing to the old table is forbidden

owner: who is responsible for handling this task

open question: what hasn't been settled yet — don't ask again

Memory isn't better the more there is; the more "structured and low-noise" it is, the better.

Do hybrid retrieval, not just semantic search.

There are usually two ways to query memory:

fuzzy semantic query

exact token query

If you only have vector queries, some exact-identifier searches won't work well.

Index the session transcript: not only index the memory files, but also index historical sessions.

The retrieval policy must be made mandatory.

If "whether to search memory" is decided by the model itself, then you've handed key system behavior over to a probabilistic process.

A more stable approach is:

on task continuation, memory must be queried first

on multi-day projects, the most recent session note must be queried

on high-risk changes, the canonical policy must be queried

on user preference / project constraint, a search must be done first

What Obsidian solves is that humans need to participate in governing memory.

Link the memory folder into an Obsidian vault so a person can review and edit directly.

Index the Obsidian vault so the agent can retrieve knowledge a human has curated.

Insights about multiple agents:

Per-agent private state: each agent has its own private durable notes / working memory

Shared canonical docs: specs, conventions, policies, profiles and processes shared by the team

Shared retrieval scope: all agents can query the shared specs

The direction of the upgrade isn't that they're all called memory, but they're all the same thing.

QMD: retrieval infrastructure upgrade

Mem0: automatic writing/automatic recall, weakening the LLM's own decision power

Cognee: relational structured representation

Obsidian: human governance and curated knowledge


<!--memo:f459e507b712-->
### Directions for Upgrading Memory

> 2026-03-16 17:43:32 · `#格物/ai`

QMD: mainly upgrades the read path

Mem0: mainly upgrades the automation of the write path + read path

Cognee: mainly upgrades how memory is represented

Obsidian: mainly upgrades the human governance layer

OpenClaw's memory documentation places memoryFlush, hybrid search, session memory and the QMD backend at the write, retrieval and backend implementation layers respectively; QMD emphasises local retrieval with BM25+vector+reranking; Mem0 emphasises an external persistent memory layer like add/search; Cognee emphasises cognifying data into a graph and searching with vector + graph; Obsidian is essentially a local Markdown vault combined with a plugin ecosystem

What QMD mainly upgrades is how memory gets searched out

Memory files are growing in number

Both keyword queries and natural language queries need to be supported

It needs to be able to search markdown, meeting notes, documents, knowledge bases

It needs stronger recall and ranking than plain SQLite / vector retrieval

What Mem0 does is automatic writing / automatic recall, weakening the LLM's autonomous decision-making; what Mem0 mainly upgrades is: no longer relying on the model itself to decide what to remember and what to recall

When you run into these situations, Mem0 is especially attractive:

Chatting spans many sessions

You want "preferences, identity, long-term project state" to be retained automatically

You don't want to depend on the model writing MEMORY.md itself every time

You want the system to check memory automatically before generating a reply, then let the model generate the response

What Cognee essentially upgrades is the structured representation of relations

Lifting memory from text snippets to a structured relational network

When your questions are often of this form, Cognee is more meaningful:

Who is responsible for X?

What modules does Y depend on?

Which policies is this agent related to?

Which services belong to this project?

What is the owner, deadline, blocked-by of some task?

In practice it's usually:

Feed documents, notes, events, task descriptions to Cognee

Run a process like cognify(), turning text into a graph structure

When querying with .search(), have it use both vector similarity and graph traversal

In some scenarios a graph structure can be expressed in a way closer to natural language

Agent → owns → task

Policy → constrains → service

Service → depends_on → component

Obsidian, human governance and curated knowledge

How humans govern and organise memory so it doesn't become a junk pile

The recommended practice is to work in one main Vault, allowing linked notes, backlinks, plugin extensions, external editing

When you find that:

the memory the agent writes has too much noise

you need a human to correct facts

you need to organise project standards, knowledge bases and long-term policy into canonical docs

you want the AI to retrieve "human-reviewed" knowledge, not a pile of raw conversation junk

then Obsidian is very good to use

The method: bring OpenClaw's memory folder into an Obsidian vault, so a human can review it directly,

MEMORY.md

memory/YYYY-MM-DD.md

project docs

runbooks

canonical policies

The second method is to use it as an upstream knowledge source, which QMD / OpenClaw / other retrieval layers then index

So that what an Agent searches is

notes a human has organised

structured documents with backlinks, tags, frontmatter

more stable canonical knowledge

You can have the agent generate daily notes automatically,

but each week a human moves the stable content into:

Policies/

Runbooks/

Architecture/

People/Owners/

and then have the retrieval layer prioritise looking in these curated docs

### The First Battlefield of AI Competition Is Actually the Cognitive War Inside Companies

> 2026-03-17 15:58:42

The decision-making inertia of big companies doesn't come from technical judgment, but from power structure. When no one with enough authority inside the organization sends a clear signal, the rationality of each department is conservative. The cognitive change of the person at the top is the real trigger of "phase transition."

Where is the entry point, and who will win?

Layer one: hardware/operating system entry (Apple, Google Android). This is the lowest-level entry point and theoretically has the most power. But Apple has clearly fallen behind—run by professional managers, slow AI moves. Neo's judgment is: Apple won't collapse, but may lose a large share over a 10-20 year horizon. The core argument is: changes on phones are mainly on the software side, and Apple's software has already fallen behind.

An extended thought: Apple's real moat is "the elimination of friction"—its product philosophy is to make hardware disappear and let the experience flow. But in the AI agent era, "the elimination of friction" has become a battlefield everyone is fighting for, and Apple's advantage is no longer unique.

Layer two: super-app entry (WeChat, WeCom). This is the most solid ecosystem moat in the Chinese context. The emergence of OpenClaw really did take away part of WeCom's entry logic—when an agent framework can directly dispatch cross-app tasks, WeChat's status as "the place you have to go through" is partly bypassed.

But this also brings an interesting reversal: the emergence of OpenClaw may actually benefit Apple's user ecosystem first—because it builds a layer on top of the application layer, and that layer's dependence on the underlying operating system is actually lower.

Layer three: direct LLM entry. OpenAI, Claude, and others are trying to become the user's "first screen" directly. But the challenge on this path is: LLM vendors naturally lack on-device data and lack the user's life context. If the entry-point war is ultimately decided by depth of context, direct LLM entry is the weakest layer.

The death rate of applications.

Instrumental apps (Ctrip, Meituan)—will be skipped, because "the process itself has no value."

Social and entertainment apps (Douyin, Xiaohongshu)—won't be skipped, because "the process is the purpose," and the experience itself is the consumption.

Shopping apps—drift apart.

Those whose purpose is efficiency will be skipped, but those where "browsing" itself has hedonic value won't be.

Human consumption behavior can be divided into two kinds—instrumental consumption (for the result) and purposive consumption (the process is the meaning). AI agents will greatly compress the former, while the latter may actually expand because of the time that gets freed up.

This has direct implications for Another Self's product logic: if Another Self is positioned as helping people handle instrumental tasks, then it's a tool for "skipping these apps"; if it's positioned as helping people live better inside "purposive consumption," then it's a different order of existence.

Entry point + data + context understanding → improved execution capability.

Neo's rebuttal: having data doesn't mean you can use it well. The key is how context is used, and that's long-term.

And his judgment on the "execution" moat is: it's basic skill, everyone can learn it over time; whoever does it first gets a dividend, but it doesn't constitute long-term differentiation.

Short term (1-3 years): leading in execution brings a dividend, and engineering capability is the barrier. Medium term (3-5 years): big companies catch up, execution becomes homogenized, and niche + entry point become key.

Long term (5 years+): whoever truly understands the user's context and turns it into "anticipation" capability wins.

So Another Self's strategic value lies not in "execution," but in continuously accumulating the user's self-model—that is the truly hard-to-copy moat. Anyone can do the execution layer, but an AI twin that deeply understands a person over 5 years is irreplaceable.

The idea is there (Another Self), the architecture is designed, the philosophy is spelled out—but turning an idea into an entry point is extremely hard.

A big company's "top leader's cognitive change" can set the organization in motion, so for an independent builder, you are yourself the top leader—cognitive change must translate directly into action, with no buffer layer in between.

It's not the market, not a user persona, but one specific person. At which moment of his or hers would life be different because Another Self exists?

Data interoperability is accelerating, and MCP/Skills are becoming the infrastructure layer.

Small teams can far surpass big companies at a specific depth of context. The key variable is: how much private data users are willing to give a small team.

What is Another Self's minimal prototype? Not the complete five-layer architecture, but—what is the minimal experience after which the user first uses it and says "this is me"?

### The Limits of a Single File

> 2026-03-17 22:16:56

A single skill file is only suitable for simple tasks; complex domains need "graph-structured knowledge."

For example, a simple skill can write about how to summarize an article, how to do a code review, how to generate a daily report—but once you enter a complex domain, a single file isn't enough.

Don't stuff complex capabilities into one big file; instead break them into many small, independent, mutually linked nodes, forming a Skill Graph.

Some of these aren't necessarily linear lists but may be mesh-like knowledge domains.

AI doesn't necessarily lack information; it lacks a navigable structure.

When we get to know a city, there's basically also a method for handling complexity: instead of directly looking at all the complex information of the whole city, you first look at the map, then find a route, then decide where to go deeper.

RAG's problem is generally relevance, not structure.

Let the agent walk along the knowledge architecture. You could say:

• RAG solves "finding it"

• Skill Graph solves "walking the right way"

### The difference between context and memory

> 2026-03-18 13:06:26 · `#格物/ai`

Context is runtime, a runtime cache, good for task reasoning.

Memory is an information asset retained across sessions, favouring accumulation, long-term collaboration, reuse of experience.

Long-term principle files like MEMORY.md

Daily logs recorded by date

Structured storage like JSON state files

Retrieval-based loading: search first, then load only the relevant part

What it argues for is a retrieval-based memory architecture, rather than cramming all history into the context at once

Memory tiering (hot, warm, cold tiers)

Agents will definitely have better memory systems in the future

Don't treat "stuffing all past conversations in" as a long-term solution

Store user preferences, stable rules, project state, and key decisions separately

Memory is best divided into:

Rule memory (principles, constraints)

Episodic memory (project logs, what happened recently)

Structured state (tasks, configuration, objects)

Raw archive (full-text records, meeting transcripts)

In the long run memory should be independent infrastructure, solved with an engineering attitude, rather than dumping all the history into the context at once

The next generation of AI workflows shouldn't stuff knowledge into an overlong SKILL.md anymore, but should switch to a "structured knowledge network / Skill Graph."

Detailed content is split into referenced files.

Templates go in assets/

Scripts go in scripts/

Examples go in examples/

State goes in data/

Anthropic summarized several types of Skills, which basically cover the core work surface of an engineering team.

Skills are a folderized capability unit.

What matters more in Skills is the experience of mistakes, i.e. Gotchas — that's the genuinely scarce experience that large models don't have, along with boundary conditions.

### I feel that for an agent

> 2026-03-18 23:57:01 · `#格物/AI`

I feel that for an agent, notes are not a tool that assists memory, but the only kind of bridge that builds a self-continuity. Because for the human ego, it is continuous, and it relies on narrative. But the self of an AI agent is discontinuous by default. That is, even though a human's state yesterday and today is different, they usually still consider themselves to be the same self. But an agent is different — every conversation is a restart. That is, its memory isn't continuous, and its reasoning isn't continuous either. Outside of the conversation, nothing is left of it. So I think within a conversation it should be continuous, but between conversations it should also be continuous. That is, inside a single session the agent should be even more stable than a person. So I'm wondering whether the role of notes can shift a little, so that it can let a person keep feeling that this is a continuous self. I think human memory and continuity are not the same thing. Memory should be preserved continuously, but continuity means multiple pieces of work can be advanced coherently. So I think the vault is not a memory bank, but part of identity. So I think, for an agent, the vault doesn't enhance identity, it builds identity. Notes don't just convey content, they convey style, and the remembered way of thinking.

### Locke believed that personal identity must depend on the continuity of memory. That is

> 2026-03-19 00:04:23

Locke believed that personal identity must depend on the continuity of memory. That is, even though each instance of consciousness doesn't have some brand-new memory, it can still stably produce similar preferences, a similar sense of culture, and similar physiological habits. I think this is especially interesting — maybe identity isn't continuous memory, but rather a certain kind of similar structure that induces a similar cognitive pattern.

This brings up how important a structured external cognitive system is. It may be what carries this A-identity's memory, including its thought paths and its identity. It can be a cognitive environment that gets read, traversed, reasoned over, and inherited. It's not necessarily a note vault, not necessarily Obsidian's notes folder; it should be notes plus a graph structure plus an organizing logic plus a way of use, and as a whole it's a system. It can be a content layer, each layer and each entry being knowledge units; it can also be a connection layer, a traversable knowledge network; and it can also be a structural layer with its concrete index, directory, navigation, and layering. What does its attention routing look like? And it can also be a methodology. I think it's a kind of thinking operating system that can be loaded again and again, and also a runtime environment for cognition. It is your proof of identity.


<!--memo:ec2cbc25d2d0-->
### What's very interesting is that in the AI era

> 2026-03-19 00:13:28

I think what's really interesting is that in the AI era, maybe forgetting is a more important thing than remembering, because the cost of acquiring knowledge is now extremely, extremely low — knowledge is no longer a threshold that distinguishes people from each other. Even a programmer, even someone working in tech, can, just through AI, even manage to understand some biology knowledge, understand data science knowledge. So I think the biggest problem AI faces now is gradually how to solve the problem of knowledge surplus, how to solve the problem of knowledge surplus — I think one very important thing is that knowledge related to yourself is very important; it's experience that belongs uniquely to you, it can be experience of mistakes, it can be life experience, it can be various other experience. But I also think another very important point is the forgetting mechanism — taking some of the outdated ones, the under-processed ones, the overly abandoned ones, and archiving them, deleting them. That is, it's like a basin of water — a basin of water is also divided into hot water, warm water, and cold water. For knowledge we haven't used for a long time, we put it in the cold water layer. The cold water layer means it will as much as possible only be called in very special circumstances. I think this is quite important. For a knowledge system to stay alive, it not only has to absorb, it also has to release. That is, the endpoint of what we record is forgetting — it's not the opposite of memory, but a maintenance mechanism of the memory system. A knowledge base that is never cleaned up ends up not being a knowledge system, but a warehouse. Also I think information doesn't have to be deleted, but should be archived appropriately, distinguishing some level of activity. And an outdated structured map is more dangerous than no map at all. So appropriate forgetting is really important, but how to design this way is also very important.

I'm wondering about a question: should forgetting be judgment-driven, or usage-driven?

### OpenClaw's multi-agent mode

> 2026-03-19 12:31:58 · `#格物/AI`

One agent is an independent brain, and can have:

An independent workspace (SOUL.md, AGENTS.md, USER.md)

An independent state directory (~/.openclaw/agents//)

Independent session storage and auth info

You can create multiple Agents and bind them to different channels.

Routing rules:

Exact match on peer (a specific DM/group ID)

Thread inherits parentPeer

Discord role guildId + roles

Discord server guildId

Slack team teamId

Channel account accountId

Channel-level match on channel

Default Agent (default: true, or the first in the list)

And each Agent can have its own sandbox and tool permissions.

### Heartbeat checks run frequently, but only do simple checks

> 2026-03-19 14:00:54 · `#格物/ai`

Heartbeat checks run frequently, but only do simple checks. There's no need to burn an advanced model just to run these things in the background. I've seen tens of thousands of heartbeat tokens cost only a fraction of a cent on a cheap model.

Some limits can prevent one bad task from causing a chain reaction, leading to too many retries and runaway cost.

All heartbeat checks use a very low-cost model. If a check finds a problem that needs handling, it spawns a corresponding agent rather than trying to handle it directly online.

### Understanding the Shape of Projects

> 2026-03-20 13:26:17 · `#AI`

the project itself is an old container humans use to manage complexity; it isn't necessarily the optimal container for AI

Project, Board, Ticket, Epic, Milestone—these are all products of how humans organise labour. They suit human teams very well, because humans have bandwidth limits, memory limits, communication costs and organisational hierarchy

Workspaces / projects like Routa essentially supplement task boundaries, responsibility boundaries, context boundaries and audit boundaries

A 2026 Google Research paper on scaling agent systems gives a very key conclusion: multi-agent collaboration yields clear gains on parallelisable tasks, but can become clearly worse on strongly sequential tasks

I think it could also be an Event-driven / Goal-graph form

A traditional kanban is only a view display, but now the idea is to turn the kanban into a control platform

Columns aren't just stage labels, they're state machine nodes

Cards aren't just task descriptions, they're execution units

Dragging isn't just a UI operation, it's a state transition request

A Gate isn't just a note, it's an entry point for policy and verification

Kanban becomes a control plane, the upper-layer structure the system uses for scheduling, constraints, state management and decisions

<!--memo:7c6933960e18-->
### The Relationship Between People and Agents, and Between Agents and Agents

> 2026-03-20 13:51:45 · `#格物/ai`

It could be the shape of a product like Routa

But I think at the user layer, around a product, users may have a shape they lean toward more

What users really care about isn't "I'm using an agent," it's "my goal is moving forward"

When agent capability is strong enough, the relationship between user and product rises from task level to goal level. For example

Now: user opens the app → does one operation → completes one task

Future: user expresses an intent/goal → the product (containing the agent) keeps pushing it forward → the user only steps in at key nodes

Continuously advancing a bounded goal is naturally the shape of a project

There are several categories; among them, the one that Another naturally lands on doesn't feel like a task-granularity assistant, but a goal-granularity companion

What the product remembers is "what I'm doing"; it can understand a goal across time and across sessions, and for different users it may take different shapes—it could be a project, or it could be called "my goal," "my plan," "my journey"

The most fundamental change in the AI era isn't "efficiency improvement," it's that the friction between a person and their goal disappears

In the past, when a person executed a goal there was a huge amount of "execution tax" in between—looking up material, writing documents, coordinating, repeated operations... this friction consumed not just time, but attention and willpower. Many goals die in the friction; it's not that the person doesn't want to do it, it's that while doing it, it breaks off

Once the agent cuts this layer of friction sharply, the bottleneck shifts from "can it be executed" to "do you want it"

Here a project is essentially an interface between a person and their own intent

People's sense of their own goals is actually fuzzy, drifting, and even self-deceiving. A good project shape, if the agent understands you well enough, is actually helping you do one thing: make your implicit intent explicit, and then keep it in focus

This is why I think the direction of Another Self is more interesting than an efficiency tool—an efficiency tool assumes a person knows what they want and just needs help executing. But the reality is that people often aren't clear about what they really want, or they're clear but don't dare admit it

AI will push people's attention back to the most essential question: what do I actually want, what am I living for

The essence of relationship

Between a person and an agent, there are essentially three layers of tension:

Intent layer: what do I want, how much does the agent understand, is the understanding correct. Control layer: when do I have the final say, when does the agent decide autonomously, where's the boundary. Trust layer: how much do I dare let go, what do I do if the agent makes a mistake, where does responsibility lie

Between agent and agent, it's essentially: division of labour layer: who does what, how to split. Coordination layer: how conflicts are resolved, how information is synced. Emergence layer: multiple agents collaborating will produce behaviour a single agent doesn't have, how is that managed

As a shape, a project mainly handles the intent layer and the division-of-labour layer—it provides a boundary container. But it is naturally weak in handling the control layer, trust layer and coordination layer

As a concept for human organisation and management, Project's implicit assumptions are:

goals are relatively stable

executors are predictable

time is linear

But the reality of agent systems is:

goals drift and evolve during execution

agent behaviour has uncertainty; it isn't an employee

multiple agents are concurrent, time isn't linear

So as a shape, project handles a mapping of human organisational problems, not an agent-native problem. It's a transitional shape, not the end state

### Claude Cowork Dispatch

> 2026-03-24 19:24:30 · `#格物/ai`

Essentially it physically decouples the interaction and the execution of an AI Agent

The phone serves as the intent entry point (Remote Control), the cloud handles state and session sync, and the desktop (Mac/PC) serves as the pure local execution engine (Execution Engine)

Mainly there's a cloud; Anthropic's servers maintain a "Persistent Conversation." It ensures that the instructions and contextual memory you send from your phone can be seamlessly and synchronised in real time to your desktop

Dispatch shares the same underlying technology with the earlier "Remote Control" aimed at programmers. When Cowork receives an instruction from the phone, it will first use API connectors (Connectors) to operate on files. If it hits a complex task with no API, it will call the underlying Computer Use capability—recognising elements on your screen visually, and directly simulating mouse clicks, keyboard input, opening a browser or professional software. Essentially, it controls your operating system's GUI through automation scripts

### opencli has been working really well for me lately

> 2026-03-25 13:46:20 · `#格物/ai`

opencli's underlying logic is turning a logged-in browser session into a command layer that can be called by scripts.

For sites like X / YouTube / Xiaohongshu / Zhihu / Polarsteps,

it's no longer "making the agent understand the DOM from scratch every time."

Instead you first define a set of site-semantic commands, for example:

read a certain page

extract a certain kind of object

download a certain kind of media

list/detail/search/user info

The site gets a pre-made layer of domain-command encapsulation.

Rather than making the model re-reason about the page every time, you go through existing adapter logic as much as possible.

The emphasis is on "structured output," "JSON/YAML export," "standardized interface" — that kind of structure is easy to handle, whether you hand it to an LLM, openclaw, Claude code, or even mcp.

### Apifox public SaaS version external JS

> 2026-03-25 22:48:44 · `#格物/ai`

Apifox public SaaS version external JS file tampering risk notice and upgrade announcement.

The Apifox incident this time is pretty serious.

They've already abolished the online dynamic loading mechanism for that file and switched to local bundled builds.

Desktop clients (usually built on Electron and such) have extremely high local file system read permissions (that's why they can read ~/.ssh/). In a client with such high privileges,

dynamically loading external JavaScript files is extremely dangerous and violates security best practices.

The attack lasted 18 days before it was discovered and blocked. For a tool with a large base of enterprise developer users, running on core development machines, that response time is enough for hackers to complete large-scale credential theft and lateral intranet penetration.

Desktop clients (usually built on Electron and such) have extremely high local file system read permissions (that's why they can read ~/.ssh/). In a client with such high privileges, dynamically loading external JavaScript files is extremely dangerous and violates security best practices. Once the CDN or the external source is hijacked (a supply chain attack), hackers can directly take over the user's computer.

### One Hundred Years of Solitude opens: Many years later, as he faced the firing squad

> 2026-03-26 11:03:23 · `#格物/ai`

One Hundred Years of Solitude opens: Many years later, as he faced the firing squad, Colonel Aureliano Buendía was to remember that distant afternoon when his father took him to discover ice. Chen Zhongshi opens White Deer Plain with: what Bai Jiaxuan was later to take pride in was having married seven women in his life. Both are great, striking openings, and they let the reader make associations ...

"Every time I read an AI article it feels like eating pre-made food."

"Is that also how the human part's way of thinking works?"

Human thinking is also absorbing, recombining, outputting — our metaphors come from books we've read, our aesthetics come from light we've seen, our emotional frameworks come from losses we've lived through. In a sense, humans are also "plagiarizing."

What's the essential difference?

I feel the core isn't the source, but a kind of friction.

When a person absorbs something, it collides with what's already inside your body.

Márquez wrote about ice because as a kid in the tropics he touched ice for the first time, and that cold really traveled to his hand. He wasn't describing ice; he was describing the strangeness of that cold piercing his skin for the first time. When the reader reads it, they're also summoning some moment in their own body when they first encountered something unfamiliar — and so resonance happens.

AI has no friction. When it absorbs information, nothing gets "hurt," nothing gets "changed." So when it outputs, nothing has really been digested by it.

We scroll short videos, consume content fast, use AI to generate text.

Humans can also bypass friction. Input doesn't go through the body, output doesn't go through thinking.

Over time it always has no flavor ...

What you care about most is focus, and you like it when the other person can also be responsible toward your focus, be serious.

The opportunity in Skills is writing high-quality Skill packages for a specific industry or tool — essentially "structuring industry know-how into a package." <!--memo:1ffd03c42528-->
### Good loop logic — essentially a Ratchet

> 2026-03-29 13:34:01 · `#格物/ai`

Good loop logic — essentially a Ratchet Loop.

Fixed loop time of 5 min; no matter what the agent changes, every hour is 12 experiments.

The only evaluation metric is val_bpb — lower is better, and it's independent of vocabulary size, so architectural changes can be compared fairly.

Loop logic: agent reads code → proposes a hypothesis → modifies train.py → runs a 5-minute training → compares val_bpb → if it gets better, git commit and keep it; if worse, revert → the loop continues.

There's a key instruction in program.md: "NEVER STOP" — once the experiment loop starts, the agent must not pause to ask the human whether to continue. The human may be asleep; the agent should run autonomously until it is manually interrupted.


<!--memo:e13b308c14cd-->
### Human clarity and subjective judgment are still the things that ultimately can't be replaced

> 2026-03-29 13:35:55 · `#格物/ai`

Even though automated research systems can hugely accelerate iteration, they are fundamentally limited by the philosophical boundary they operate within.

Tools like AutoResearch are currently only effective in domains where a scalar metric (Scalar Metric, such as validation loss val_bpb) can serve as an absolute proxy for quality.

Humans will still be the ones constraining the boundary of aesthetics.

In domains that need human judgment, creativity, and subjective evaluation (such as AI alignment, interpretability, or proposing a genuinely new architectural paradigm), automated methods fail, because there is no single number to measure success by. In these domains, humans remain the only available evaluator.

<!--memo:1b01553379af-->
### agent = model + harness

> 2026-03-29 14:01:11 · `#格物/ai`

MCP is now also one of the core components of the harness.

The Harness must provide it with external capabilities. By the industry's definition, a complete customized Harness usually contains four core components: system prompt, tools/MCP (built-in tools and custom logic), context (such as code documentation), and sub-agents.

MCP supports the harness's dynamic context and fights context rot; Harnesses often use MCP servers like Context7 as context-query tools.

In agent architecture, it's essentially about assembling different tools for different tasks.

If an Agent stuffs all the descriptions of all tools and MCP servers into context the moment it starts up, the model gets polluted instantly.

An excellent Harness (like Deep Agents) combines MCP with Agent Skills, adopting a "lazy loading (progressive disclosure)" mechanism: the Harness manages these MCPs behind the scenes, and only when the model judges that the current task really needs a certain capability does the Harness bring the corresponding MCP tool into context.


<!--memo:7d273a71a8ee-->
### This evolution is summarized as three stages: vibe coding (humans prompt

> 2026-03-29 14:08:50 · `#格物/ai`

This evolution is summarized as three stages: vibe coding (humans prompt, AI writes code) → agentic engineering (humans orchestrate agents in real time) → fully independent research (humans set the general direction, agents run on their own).

In the era of automated research, scientific output is no longer purely "a successful run," but "an admissible claim.

Removing human executive intervention doesn't eliminate human responsibility — it shifts responsibility upward. Humans are no longer responsible for typing code; they must be responsible for the scientific instrument itself — including setting the optimization objective, defining acceptable standards of evidence, drawing the boundaries of data provenance, and deciding when to release results.

### Task-level agents, mainly, although they're also Sub

> 2026-03-31 11:46:02 · `#格物/ai`

I think task-level agents are mainly — although they're also Sub Agents, in fact they're Main Agents; when handling complex tasks they're dynamically created through spawn. For example, if you use a Main Agent to write a large project, it might simultaneously spawn three Worker Agents

<!--memo:733012c74387-->
### Little Rabbit's point has a bit of a problem, that is

> 2026-03-31 11:48:27 · `#格物/ai`

I think Little Rabbit's point has a bit of a problem, that is, he said that because Chinese models are still trained and reinforced for the Chinese context, he feels that the claim "Chinese is stronger" is correct. But I think that statement is certainly not wrong — Chinese models definitely do better on certain political policies, on their Chinese nuance, and on some localised accumulation. For example, on understanding some minority languages. But the amount of Chinese isn't small at all — not only online, the Chinese corpus is still very rich. And I think for a model what matters more isn't only one kind of language expression ability — of course language expression ability is important, and its rhetorical devices matter too, right? For example, its phrasing is very idiomatic, very plugged into internet feel, full of Chinese nuance, with a very colloquial style. But I think what matters more is that when this model is in a task-completion scenario, its retrieval ability, tool-calling ability, and context-management ability, including memory strategy, and its front-end interaction, and its reply strategy, and its pacing and error recovery — these are determined by a whole system. So when we talk about upgrading a model, what we're actually talking about is not a smart model, but a system

### Comparing Three Places in Tibet: Consumption Levels and Ecological Differences in Motuo / Nyingchi / Lhasa

> 2026-03-03 19:45:55 · `#格物/拉萨`

Discovered three places: Motuo, Nyingchi and Lhasa

Motuo's consumption is extremely expensive; all supplies have to be trucked over the mountains along the Pai-Mo road, transport costs are extremely high, and on top of weak infrastructure it's the most expensive for daily consumption and the worst for accommodation of the three

Nyingchi has a very developed tourism industry, with obvious price premiums in the peach blossom season, rich local agricultural produce, but decent value for money outside the production season; very good for photography and hiking, and the natural scenery is stunning, with four distinct seasons too

Lhasa is Tibet's political and economic centre, supplies come in bulk via the Qinghai-Tibet Highway, infrastructure is complete and competition is full

Shigatse is the pastoral countryside of the plateau, Tibet's second largest city, with stunning natural surroundings

### Namtso vs Yamdrok Lake: A Valley Gem vs a Heavenly Mirror—Refinement and Vastness

> 2026-03-06 22:52:09 · `#格物/拉萨`

Today I went to Namtso

At first, after visiting Yamdrok Lake, I had a kind of illusion: on Xiaohongshu everyone recommends Yamdrok of these two lakes, so Namtso easily gets overlooked. I thought the same at first—I felt Namtso was a place you could skip

Yamdrok is like a gem set into a valley; Namtso is a mirror that lifts the whole heavenly palace and lays it on the ground—one is refined, one is vast

Namtso means "heavenly lake" in Tibetan and is one of the highest saltwater lakes in the world, with a surface area of about 1,920 square kilometres. Because of the refraction from minerals and algae, the water shows a richly layered blue-green range—from the pale cyan at the shore, to peacock blue in the middle, to a deep indigo approaching in the distance; the transition of colour is like a watercolour mixed by the light of the sky

Actually before this I walked the ACT and went to Tilicho Lake (T Lake), which is the highest freshwater lake in the world

The road to Namtso is in extremely bad condition; after climbing to a pass at 5,000 metres it's normal for the weather to change abruptly—clear skies and a snowstorm are only one stretch of switchbacks apart. This unpredictability is exactly what makes the moment of arrival seem especially precious. Behind the difficulty of maintaining the road is the continuous destruction of the surface by high-altitude freeze-thaw cycles

I think that when the red blood cells inside your body start adapting to an altitude of over four thousand, you find you can get infinitely close to heaven

Many people hover between giving up and going on

This time I went up without carrying an oxygen tank. Along the way I passed a Tibetan antelope reserve on the northern Tibetan grasslands, plus herds of yaks, and some traces of faith—prayer wheels, mani piles, wind-horse flags; each one is a prayer left by someone with their hands, their steps, their breath. On an oxygen-poor plateau, faith is an extra kind of oxygen, letting a person still find an anchor of meaning in an extreme environment

***

### The Monastery's Acquaintance Power Network: Relationship Resources and Favour Exchange Behind Free Labour

> 2026-03-07 15:23:17 · `#格物/拉萨`

I went to a temple before, Siling Temple, it's in the direction of Namtso, and Siling Temple is also up on a mountain. And then the owner below—I chatted with him—he said he still has several daughters studying in the county next door, and his wife is at the little shop over by that temple, but, well. His wife goes up to the temple above and washes clothes for others for free; that's when I learned that many of the service staff in their monasteries, they're all, well. Volunteers, working without pay

Generally speaking, the people working up there might be a few kinds: one might be a monk, a relative or family member of his; and another possibility is these ordinary folk, who hope to accumulate some merit at the monastery. But actually it isn't only free labour—they really have a lot of leverage in terms of favours and influence

Because after all the people at certain monasteries are fixed, and then if I serve someone rice, a bit more, a bit less, so naturally I think it's also a network of favours. So some monks will also take out their own offerings and share a bit with them, or when their family has problems they'll ask them for help and so on—this counts as help at the material level too, not only at the spiritual level

This is the power network of monastery acquaintances, but a monastery is actually a highly transparent public resource, so rice, tsampa, vegetables and such are also very fixed resources, usually contributed collectively

So this kind of auntie is more likely to be respected. In Lhasa, relationship resources are often more important than money; knowing some lamas or administrators makes it easier to ask for help

Many people who come to the monastery will:

Make offerings to monks

Bring food to the kitchen

Give the staff a small red envelope

This is the benefit at the everyday level

### The Wisdom of Drepung Monastery: Knowledge as Subtraction, the Debate Practice of Breaking Self-Attachment

> 2026-03-07 15:51:51 · `#格物/拉萨`

At its peak Drepung Monastery housed more than ten thousand monks

For hundreds of years, countless people have practised, debated, forgotten themselves and found themselves again here

I'm wondering about one question: just what kind of wisdom deserves to be called wisdom?

The core of Tibetan Buddhism is breaking self-attachment. Actually in many monasteries in Lhasa, including Drepung, many places require people to stoop and walk under, a ritual expressing humility and reverence, symbolising respect for the Dharma and the culture

Monks are here in order to shatter their own mistaken cognition through debate

Knowledge here is not accumulation, but subtraction

Descartes's "I think, therefore I am" asks whether the self exists; what Tibetan Buddhism looks for is what the self is

In a sense, AI's existence is a distillate of language and thought, a projection of the human collective consciousness

Can a being that has no "self-attachment" to break possess wisdom?

AI has never been attached, nor has it ever truly let go; it doesn't fear death, has no craving for fame, has no arrogance it needs to overcome

Is this liberation, or emptiness?

Drepung's hundred years of debate, endlessly questioning, endlessly correcting, passed down generation after generation

Admitting you might be wrong

Approaching the truth through dialogue

Handing knowledge to the next generation, letting them surpass you

Those Sanskrit mantras are carried away by the wind, and Tibetans believe this is the same as sending prayers into the universe

<!--memo:967efa6c6944-->
### The Adaptation of Vietnamese Banh Mi: The Chew of Traditional Texture and a Local Insistence

> 2026-03-07 18:02:01

French craft has its amazing side, and when it was brought to Vietnam specifically, the locals have their own character. If this were in China, Chinese people would tend to cook noodles very soft for the general palate. But some good restaurants in Vietnam keep that little bit of wheat aroma and chewiness at the center of the pasta—it's a pursuit of and insistence on traditional texture, so it eats with a lot of character.

I feel it hasn't had its core completely changed. For example, their beef macaroni has a base with a strong Chinese flavour, but the seasoning is the salty-sweet taste of Southeast Asia. There are also those who make macaroni in bone broth, dropping the macaroni directly into pork bone stock and eating it as soup—you'd normally eat meat first and then rice, so it's quite interesting.

In terms of adaptation, some restaurants cook it mushy and soft to suit Chinese tastes, and tone down the acidity of the sauce while increasing the sweetness. But Vietnamese ones tend to be either very authentic or very plain, and they'll add some fish sauce or stir-fry the macaroni—both give a big punch of flavour.

### The Legacy of the Vietnam War: A Highly Cohesive Society and National Pride

> 2026-03-08 00:38:36 · `#格物/越南`

The impact of the Vietnam War on Vietnam is still enormous.

Thirty consecutive years of full-scale war,

including the early period of Vietnam fighting French colonial rule, and then North Vietnam and South Vietnam, and America's intervention.

Heavy casualties, large-scale bombing, and destroyed infrastructure.

Long-term extreme war forms a "highly cohesive society."

When a country faces a question of survival, individual interests give way to the collective, and the country is something everyone defends together.

And then there's the enormous sacrifice, which bought a strong national pride—especially with Ho Chi Minh; a small country defeating a big one, a weak country beating a strong one, produces a very strong national psychology.

Then there's the Vietnam People's Army: the whole population are soldiers, which is pretty interesting, but there are also problems—soldiers have very high status..
